=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a 2D integer array intervals, where intervals[i] = [l_i, r_i, weight_i]. Interval i starts at position l_i and ends at r_i, and has a weight of weight_i. You can choose up to 4 non-overlapping intervals. The score of the chosen intervals is defined as the total sum of their weights.
Return the lexicographically smallest array of at most 4 indices from intervals with maximum score, representing your choice of non-overlapping intervals.
Two intervals are said to be non-overlapping if they do not share any points. In particular, intervals sharing a left or right boundary are considered overlapping.
An array a is lexicographically smaller than an array b if in the first position where a and b differ, array a has an element that is less than the corresponding element in b.
If the first min(a.length, b.length) elements do not differ, then the shorter array is the lexicographically smaller one.
 
Example 1:

Input: intervals = [[1,3,2],[4,5,2],[1,5,5],[6,9,3],[6,7,1],[8,9,1]]
Output: [2,3]
Explanation:
You can choose the intervals with indices 2, and 3 with respective weights of 5, and 3.

Example 2:

Input: intervals = [[5,8,1],[6,7,7],[4,7,3],[9,10,6],[7,8,2],[11,14,3],[3,5,5]]
Output: [1,3,5,6]
Explanation:
You can choose the intervals with indices 1, 3, 5, and 6 with respective weights of 7, 6, 3, and 5.

 
Constraints:

1 <= intevals.length <= 5 * 10^4
intervals[i].length == 3
intervals[i] = [l_i, r_i, weight_i]
1 <= l_i <= r_i <= 10^9
1 <= weight_i <= 10^9

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maximumWeight(self, intervals: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

We can model sorted lists as sets. Lex order of sorted lists. We want: For any sorted lists A,B with no one a prefix of the other and A<B, and any x not in A∪B, insert(A,x) < insert(B,x). Is this always true? Let's try to find counterexample using order statistics.

Let A=(a1..am), B=(b1..bn), A<B. Let k = first index where they differ; if one ends before, prefix case. So k <= min(m,n), a_k < b_k. For all t<k, a_t=b_t.

Insert x. Let C=insert(A,x), D=insert(B,x).

Consider position of x relative to k.

- If x < a_k (and x < b_k? Since a_k < b_k, x < a_k implies x < b_k). Then in both C and D, x is inserted before position k? More precisely, x may be less than some common prefix elements too. Let q = number of common prefix elements < x (same for A and B because prefix equal). Then C and D have same first q elements, then x at q+1, then after x compare A_{q+1..} and B_{q+1..}. Since x < a_k, q < k. The first differing element after x? Need if A and B differ at k, and k > q. After x, we compare A_{q+1} vs B_{q+1}. If q+1 < k, they are equal; continue. At position corresponding to k, C has a_k, D has b_k, so C<D. Good.

- If x > b_k? Then x is after position k in both? Not necessarily if there are elements before k equal and x > b_k, then x inserted after at least k elements in both. Before x, compare A and B; at position k, a_k < b_k, so C<D. Good.

- If a_k <= x <= b_k? Since a_k < b_k. Cases:
  * x = a_k? x not in A, but could equal a_k? x is original index of current interval, cannot equal previous interval index, so x not in A/B. But if a_k = x impossible. So x != a_k. Similarly x != b_k. So a_k < x < b_k.
Then in A, x is inserted after a_k (position > k); in B, x is inserted before b_k (position <= k). Compare C and D:
Before position k, common prefix. At position k:
- In C, what is at position k? Since x inserted after a_k, C_k = a_k (because before k common, a_k at k).
- In D, x inserted before b_k, and since x < b_k, D_k = x? Wait D has common prefix up to k-1, then x if x < b_k and x > previous common prefix? Need x > a_k = b? Actually common prefix before k equal; x > a_k, so x > all previous common elements. Thus in D, x is at position k (replacing b_k). So C_k = a_k, D_k = x. Since a_k < x, C<D. Good.

Thus insertion monotonic holds for non-prefix. Great.

Thus DP tie-breaking is safe.

If a state has lower score, adding same future interval yields lower total score than adding to a higher-score state (provided both compatible). Since weights positive and additive, higher previous score always dominates. So no need to keep lower score. Good.

Let's write add_idx:

```
def add_idx(t, x):
    if not t:
        return (x,)
    if x < t[0]:
        return (x,) + t
    if len(t) == 1:
        return (t[0], x)
    if x < t[1]:
        return (t[0], x, t[1])
    if len(t) == 2:
        return (t[0], t[1], x)
    if x < t[2]:
        return (t[0], t[1], x, t[2])
    if len(t) == 3:
        return (t[0], t[1], t[2], x)
    # len(t) == 4
    if x < t[3]:
        # but x > t[2]? if not handled? We handle x < t[2] before. If len=4 and x between t[2] and t[3]:
        return (t[0], t[1], t[2], x, t[3])? Wait at most 4 intervals, cannot add to length 4! Important.
```

Let's implement add_idx for len 0..3:

```
def insert_idx(t, x):
    if not t:
        return (x,)
    if x < t[0]:
        return (x,) + t
    if len(t) == 1:
        return (t[0], x)
    if x < t[1]:
        return (t[0], x, t[1])
    if len(t) == 2:
        return (t[0], t[1], x)
    # len(t) == 3
    if x < t[2]:
        return (t[0], t[1], x, t[2])
    return (t[0], t[1], t[2], x)
```

Need check if len(t)==3 and x < t[0] handled; if x between t[0] and t[1]? The first `if x < t[0]` false, len!=1, `if x < t[1]` handles. If x between t[1] and t[2], `if x < t[2]` handles. If x > t[2], return append. Good.

But what if len(t)==3 and x equals an existing index? impossible. If x < t[0] and len 3, return (x, t0, t1, t2) length4. Good.

The two previous tuples considered for a given q are from the same state dp[j-1][p_q], so they are both compatible with q. Adding q adds same index to both. Scores remain equal. Let's write code:

```
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def maximumWeight(self, intervals: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(intervals)
        arr = sorted((r, l, w, idx) for idx, (l, r, w) in enumerate(intervals))
        ends = [r for r, _, _, _ in arr]
        p_list = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, (r, l, _, _) in enumerate(arr, 1):
            p_list[i] = bisect_left(ends, l)
        
        def insert_idx(t, x):
            if not t:
                return (x,)
            if x < t[0]:
                return (x,) + t
            if len(t) == 1:
                return (t[0], x)
            if x < t[1]:
                return (t[0], x, t[1])
            if len(t) == 2:
                return (t[0], t[1], x)
            if x < t[2]:
                return (t[0], t[1], x, t[2])
            return (t[0], t[1], t[2], x)
        
        prev_score = [0] * (n + 1)
        prev_ans = [()] * (n + 1)
        
        for _ in range(4):
            cur_score = [0] * (n + 1)
            cur_ans = [()] * (n + 1)
            for i in range(1, n + 1):
                _, l, w, idx = arr[i - 1]
                p = p_list[i]
                
                inc_score = prev_score[p] + w
                inc_ans = insert_idx(prev_ans[p], idx)
                
                exc_score = cur_score[i - 1]
                exc_ans = cur_ans[i - 1]
                
                if inc_score > exc_score or (inc_score == exc_score and inc_ans < exc_ans):
                    cur_score[i] = inc_score
                    cur_ans[i] = inc_ans
                else:
                    cur_score[i] = exc_score
                    cur_ans[i] = exc_ans
            prev_score, prev_ans = cur_score, cur_ans
        
        return list(prev_ans[n])
```

Need check `insert_idx` for len(t)==3 and x < t[2] but x might be less than t[1]? We already checked `if x < t[1]` before. If len(t)==3 and x < t[1], returns (t0, x, t1) length3? Wait if len(t)==3 and x < t[1], the code at `if x < t[1]: return (t[0], x, t[1])` returns length 3, dropping t[2]! That's a bug. Need handle len(t) separately. Let's rewrite carefully.

For len(t)==3:
- if x < t[0]: (x, t0, t1, t2)
- elif x < t[1]: (t0, x, t1, t2)
- elif x < t[2]: (t0, t1, x, t2)
- else: (t0, t1, t2, x)

For len(t)==2:
- if x < t[0]: (x, t0, t1)
- elif x < t[1]: (t0, x, t1)
- else: (t0, t1, x)

For len(t)==1:
- if x < t[0]: (x, t0)
- else: (t0, x)

The previous code with sequential checks was wrong for len=3 because `if x < t[1]` returned 3-tuple. Need fix.

Implement generic based on len:

```
def insert_idx(t, x):
    if not t:
        return (x,)
    if len(t) == 1:
        if x < t[0]:
            return (x, t[0])
        return (t[0], x)
    if len(t) == 2:
        if x < t[0]:
            return (x, t[0], t[1])
        if x < t[1]:
            return (t[0], x, t[1])
        return (t[0], t[1], x)
    # len(t) == 3
    if x < t[0]:
        return (x, t[0], t[1], t[2])
    if x < t[1]:
        return (t[0], x, t[1], t[2])
    if x < t[2]:
        return (t[0], t[1], x, t[2])
    return (t[0], t[1], t[2], x)
```

This is correct. Since len(t) <=3. But what if len(t)==4? Not possible, but if defensive, we could handle len 4? But adding to len4 invalid. We can assert? No. Could write generic for any len up to 4 using loop? Simpler and less error-prone:

```
def insert_idx(t, x):
    for pos, val in enumerate(t):
        if x < val:
            return t[:pos] + (x,) + t[pos:]
    return t + (x,)
```

This handles any len, but creates slices; len <=3, fine. This is much simpler and less bug-prone. 200k calls, tuple slicing length <=4, okay. Use this. It will also handle len 4 if ever (would produce len5, but not). But if len=4, at most 4? Actually if called with len=4, it would produce 5, invalid. But not called. Could add if len(t) == 4: return t? But that would be wrong if called. Better not. The loop version is concise and correct for len <=3. Let's use loop version to avoid manual bugs. Performance: 200k * up to 4 iterations = 800k, trivial. It creates tuple via concatenation; okay.

```
def insert_idx(t, x):
    for pos, val in enumerate(t):
        if x < val:
            return t[:pos] + (x,) + t[pos:]
    return t + (x,)
```

Case x > b_k: Then x inserted after position k in both? Need if some elements before k > x? No, prefix elements < a_k < b_k < x, so all before k < x. Elements after k may be < x or > x. - In C, before position k are common prefix; C_k = a_k (because x after a_k).
- In D, before position k are common prefix; D_k = x (because x before b_k and after prefix).

Now, let's think about if there is a possible issue with "up to 4" and if the DP should consider exactly 4 intervals because "up to 4" maybe
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
from bisect import bisect_left

class Solution:
    def maximumWeight(self, intervals: List[List[int]]) -> List[int]:
        n = len(intervals)

        # Sort by right endpoint. Keep the original index for the answer.
        arr = sorted((r, l, w, idx) for idx, (l, r, w) in enumerate(intervals))
        ends = [r for r, _, _, _ in arr]

        # p[i] = number of intervals whose right endpoint is < l_i
        p = [0] * (n + 1)
        for i, (_, l, _, _) in enumerate(arr, 1):
            p[i] = bisect_left(ends, l)

        def insert_idx(t: tuple, x: int) -> tuple:
            """Insert original index x into sorted tuple t."""
            for pos, val in enumerate(t):
                if x < val:
                    return t[:pos] + (x,) + t[pos:]
            return t + (x,)

        # dp for 0 chosen intervals
        prev_score = [0] * (n + 1)
        prev_ans = [()] * (n + 1)

        for _ in range(4):
            cur_score = [0] * (n + 1)
            cur_ans = [()] * (n + 1)

            for i in range(1, n + 1):
                _, l, w, idx = arr[i - 1]
                before = p[i]

                inc_score = prev_score[before] + w
                inc_ans = insert_idx(prev_ans[before], idx)

                exc_score = cur_score[i - 1]
                exc_ans = cur_ans[i - 1]

                if inc_score > exc_score or (inc_score == exc_score and inc_ans < exc_ans):
                    cur_score[i] = inc_score
                    cur_ans[i] = inc_ans
                else:
                    cur_score[i] = exc_score
                    cur_ans[i] = exc_ans

            prev_score, prev_ans = cur_score, cur_ans

        return list(prev_ans[n])
```
